Yeah… I do, I guess. I try not to make a big thing of it, but I’ve been… sort of working around it.

Like, I’ll hand off height inspections to one of my foremen if I can, or move a meeting down to ground level instead of having it up on an open floor. And if I really have to go up, I usually keep someone right beside me and just get through it as fast as I can.

Even when I can see the rails are there and I know the setup is supposed to be safe.  \n\nI know that probably sounds irrational, but it’s gotten hard enough that I figured I should come talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Is it only heights that trigger this feeling, or are there other situations that make you feel like you're in danger?\n\nPatient: It’s really just the heights, as far as I can tell. Like scaffolds, roof edges, upper platforms — that kind of thing. I can look at the railings and know, logically, that they’re there and it’s supposed to be safe, but my head still jumps to, “I’m going to go over.” I don’t really get that same danger feeling in other situations, not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Has this fear of heights started to affect your work or daily activities in any way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’ve tried to keep it from being a big deal, but it is starting to show at work. They’ve started questioning some of my calls, which is… not great.\n\nAnd, um, my employer’s insurer actually pushed for an occupational health review because I wasn’t completing some key safety inspections. I don’t want it to sound like I’m not doing my job, but that’s kind of where it’s gotten.\n\nDoctor: When you're in a situation that triggers this fear of heights, do you ever have episodes where you feel an intense fear or discomfort that reaches a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t want to overstate it, but it can come on pretty fast. Like the second I step onto temporary stairs, or even just start walking toward an open upper floor, my chest tightens up and my hands get sweaty.\n\nSometimes it’s like my legs are going to lock before I can make myself take another step. It usually feels like it spikes right away, within a few minutes at most.\n\nDoctor: When you're in a situation that triggers this fear, are you worried about falling or is it more about losing control of yourself?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly about falling, I think. Like I’m about to go over.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you've ever had what you would describe as a panic attack in these situations near heights?\n\nPatient: I guess… yeah, maybe. I don’t know if I’d call it that exactly, but it feels pretty close.\n\nThe second I step onto temporary stairs, or even just start walking toward an open upper floor, my chest tightens up, my hands get wet, and I get this feeling like my legs might lock up before I can make myself take another step. I’m not trying to be dramatic, but it can hit really fast.\n\nDoctor: Is the fear you experience near heights ever about something other than falling?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s falling… or that feeling like I’m going to go over. I don’t think it’s really about anything else.\n\nLike, if I’m near a scaffold, a roof edge, or an upper platform, my head just tells me, “You’re about to go over,” even when I can see the rails are there and I know it’s supposed to be safe. I know that probably doesn’t sound totally rational, but that’s where my mind goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself able to be near heights without this fear occurring?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, I try to tell myself I’m fine, especially because I should be used to job sites by now. I know that probably sounds like a lot, but that’s usually what happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you actively avoid situations that involve heights because of this fear?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, I guess. I try not to make a big thing of it, but I’ve been… sort of working around it.\n\nLike, I’ll hand off height inspections to one of my foremen if I can, or move a meeting down to ground level instead of having it up on an open floor. And if I really have to go up, I usually keep someone right beside me and just get through it as fast as I can.\n\nI know that probably isn’t ideal for my job, but I’m still getting the work done… or trying to, anyway.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this fear of heights?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over a year, I guess. Since that stair tower accident — about thirteen months ago now.\n\nI kept thinking it would settle down on its own, so I probably didn’t treat it like a big thing at first, but yeah… that’s when it started.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. But the second I step onto temporary stairs or even start walking toward an open upper floor, it kind of kicks in.\n\nMy chest tightens, my hands get sweaty, and I get this feeling like my legs might lock up before I can take another step. I know that probably sounds like a lot, but that’s usually what happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you actively avoid situations that involve heights because of this fear?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, I guess. I try not to make a big thing of it, but I’ve been… sort of working around it.\n\nLike, I’ll hand off height inspections to one of my foremen if I can, or move a meeting down to ground level instead of having it up on an open floor. And if I really have to go up, I usually keep someone right beside me and just get through it as fast as I can.\n\nI know that probably isn’t ideal for my job, but I’m still getting the work done… or trying to, anyway.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this fear of heights?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over a year, I guess. Since that stair tower accident — about thirteen months ago now.\n\nI kept thinking it would settle down on its own, so I probably didn’t treat it like a big thing at first, but yeah… that’s when it started.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned a stair tower accident about thirteen months ago, did that incident trigger your fear of heights?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to overstate it, but that’s really when it started.\n\nBefore that accident, heights were just part of the job. Since then — for about thirteen months now — it’s been different. I kept hoping it was just a phase or something I’d get over, but it’s been going on since then.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.228, F41.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, it’s a little embarrassing, honestly. I work construction, and lately when I have to get near a scaffold, or a roof edge, or one of the upper platforms, my head just kind of tells me I’m about to go over. Even when I can see the rails are there and I know the setup is supposed to be safe.  \n\nI know that probably sounds irrational, but it’s gotten hard enough that I figured I should come talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Is it only heights that trigger this feeling, or are there other situations that make you feel like you're in danger?\n\nPatient: It’s really just the heights, as far as I can tell. Like scaffolds, roof edges, upper platforms — that kind of thing. I can look at the railings and know, logically, that they’re there and it’s supposed to be safe, but my head still jumps to, “I’m going to go over.” I don’t really get that same danger feeling in other situations, not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Has this fear of heights started to affect your work or daily activities in any way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’ve tried to keep it from being a big deal, but it is starting to show at work. I’ve missed or put off some of the upper-level walk-throughs, and that’s caused some issues with the crews and subcontractors. They’ve started questioning some of my calls, which is… not great.\n\nAnd, um, my employer’s insurer actually pushed for an occupational health review because I wasn’t completing some key safety inspections. I don’t want it to sound like I’m not doing my job, but that’s kind of where it’s gotten.\n\nDoctor: When you're in a situation that triggers this fear of heights, do you ever have episodes where you feel an intense fear or discomfort that reaches a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t want to overstate it, but it can come on pretty fast. Like the second I step onto temporary stairs, or even just start walking toward an open upper floor, my chest tightens up and my hands get sweaty.\n\nSometimes it’s like my legs are going to lock before I can make myself take another step. It usually feels like it spikes right away, within a few minutes at most.\n\nDoctor: When you're in a situation that triggers this fear, are you worried about falling or is it more about losing control of yourself?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly about falling, I think. Or… not exactly falling because something actually fails, but my mind goes straight to, “I’m going to go over,” even if I can see the rails are there and I know it’s supposed to be safe.\n\nI don’t really want to sound dramatic, but near a scaffold or roof edge or an upper platform, that’s the thought that hits me. Like I’m about to go over.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you've ever had what you would describe as a panic attack in these situations near heights?\n\nPatient: I guess… yeah, maybe. I don’t know if I’d call it that exactly, but it feels pretty close.\n\nThe second I step onto temporary stairs, or even just start walking toward an open upper floor, my chest tightens up, my hands get wet, and I get this feeling like my legs might lock up before I can make myself take another step. I’m not trying to be dramatic, but it can hit really fast.\n\nDoctor: Is the fear you experience near heights ever about something other than falling?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s falling… or that feeling like I’m going to go over. I don’t think it’s really about anything else.\n\nLike, if I’m near a scaffold, a roof edge, or an upper platform, my head just tells me, “You’re about to go over,” even when I can see the rails are there and I know it’s supposed to be safe. I know that probably doesn’t sound totally rational, but that’s where my mind goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself able to be near heights without this fear occurring?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, I try to tell myself I’m fine, especially because I should be used to job sites by now. But the second I step onto temporary stairs or even start walking toward an open upper floor, it kind of kicks in.\n\nMy chest tightens, my hands get sweaty, and I get this feeling like my legs might lock up before I can take another step. And if I really have to go up, I usually keep someone right beside me and just get through it as fast as I can.\n\nI know that probably isn’t ideal for my job, but I’m still getting the work done… or trying to, anyway.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this fear of heights?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over a year, I guess. Since that stair tower accident — about thirteen months ago now.\n\nI kept thinking it would settle down on its own, so I probably didn’t treat it like a big thing at first, but yeah… that’s when it started.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned a stair tower accident about thirteen months ago, did that incident trigger your fear of heights?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to overstate it, but that’s really when it started.\n\nBefore that accident, heights were just part of the job. Since then — for about thirteen months now — it’s been different. I kept hoping it was just a phase or something I’d get over, but it’s been going on since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time, lasting at least a few months, where you didn't experience this fear of heights?\n\nPatient: No… not really. I mean, I wish I could say there was a stretch where it went away, but since that stair tower accident, it’s kind of just been there.\n\nMaybe some days are easier than others, but I don’t think I’ve had a few months where I wasn’t dealing with it. It’s been about thirteen months now.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.     "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.228, F41.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, it’s a little embarrassing, honestly. I work construction, and lately when I have to get near a scaffold, or a roof edge, or one of the upper platforms, my head just kind of tells me I’m about to go over. Even when I can see the rails are there and I know the setup is supposed to be safe.  \n\nI know that probably sounds irrational, but it’s gotten hard enough that I figured I should come talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Is it only heights that trigger this feeling, or are there other situations that make you feel like you're in danger?\n\nPatient: It’s really just the heights, as far as I can tell. Like scaffolds, roof edges, upper platforms — that kind of thing. I can look at the railings and know, logically, that they’re there and it’s supposed to be safe, but my head still jumps to, “I’m going to go over.” I don’t really get that same danger feeling in other situations, not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Has this fear of heights started to affect your work or daily activities in any way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’ve tried to keep it from being a big deal, but it is starting to show at work. I’ve missed or put off some of the upper-level walk-throughs, and that’s caused some issues with the crews and subcontractors. They’ve started questioning some of my calls, which is… not great.\n\nAnd, um, my employer’s insurer actually pushed for an occupational health review because I wasn’t completing some key safety inspections. I don’t want it to sound like I’m not doing my job, but that’s kind of where it’s gotten.\n\nDoctor: When you're in a situation that triggers this fear of heights, do you ever have episodes where you feel an intense fear or discomfort that reaches a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t want to overstate it, but it can come on pretty fast. Like the second I step onto temporary stairs, or even just start walking toward an open upper floor, my chest tightens up and my hands get sweaty.\n\nSometimes it’s like my legs are going to lock before I can make myself take another step. It usually feels like it spikes right away, within a few minutes at most.\n\nDoctor: When you're in a situation that triggers this fear, are you worried about falling or is it more about losing control of yourself?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly about falling, I think. Or… not exactly falling because something actually fails, but my mind goes straight to, “I’m going to go over,” even if I can see the rails are there and I know it’s supposed to be safe.\n\nI don’t really want to sound dramatic, but near a scaffold or roof edge or an upper platform, that’s the thought that hits me. Like I’m about to go over.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you've ever had what you would describe as a panic attack in these situations near heights?\n\nPatient: I guess… yeah, maybe. I don’t know if I’d call it that exactly, but it feels pretty close.\n\nThe second I step onto temporary stairs, or even just start walking toward an open upper floor, my chest tightens up, my hands get wet, and I get this feeling like my legs might lock up before I can make myself take another step. I’m not trying to be dramatic, but it can hit really fast.\n\nDoctor: Is the fear you experience near heights ever about something other than falling?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s falling… or that feeling like I’m going to go over. I don’t think it’s really about anything else.\n\nLike, if I’m near a scaffold, a roof edge, or an upper platform, my head just tells me, “You’re about to go over,” even when I can see the rails are there and I know it’s supposed to be safe. I know that probably doesn’t sound totally rational, but that’s where my mind goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself able to be near heights without this fear occurring?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, I try to tell myself I’m fine, especially because I should be used to job sites by now. But the second I step onto temporary stairs or even start walking toward an open upper floor, it kind of kicks in.\n\nMy chest tightens, my hands get sweaty, and I get this feeling like my legs might lock up before I can take another step. I know that probably sounds like a lot, but that’s usually what happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you actively avoid situations that involve heights because of this fear?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, I guess. I try not to make a big thing of it, but I’ve been… sort of working around it.\n\nLike, I’ll hand off height inspections to one of my foremen if I can, or move a meeting down to ground level instead of having it up on an open floor. And if I really have to go up, I usually keep someone right beside me and just get through it as fast as I can.\n\nI know that probably isn’t ideal for my job, but I’m still getting the work done… or trying to, anyway.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this fear of heights?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over a year, I guess. Since that stair tower accident — about thirteen months ago now.\n\nI kept thinking it would settle down on its own, so I probably didn’t treat it like a big thing at first, but yeah… that’s when it started.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned a stair tower accident about thirteen months ago, did that incident trigger your fear of heights?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to overstate it, but that’s really when it started.\n\nBefore that accident, heights were just part of the job. Since then — for about thirteen months now — it’s been different. I kept hoping it was just a phase or something I’d get over, but it’s been going on since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time, lasting at least a few months, where you didn't experience this fear of heights?\n\nPatient: No… not really. I mean, I wish I could say there was a stretch where it went away, but since that stair tower accident, it’s kind of just been there.\n\nMaybe some days are easier than others, but I don’t think I’ve had a few months where I wasn’t dealing with it. It’s been about thirteen months now.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of heights been present for most days since it started about thirteen months ago?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s fair to say. Most days, yes.\n\nI don’t like making it sound bigger than it is, but since that stair tower accident about thirteen months ago, it’s been pretty consistently there. Some days I manage around it better, but I don’t think it’s really gone away.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think… mostly it’s about falling, honestly. Or at least that’s how it feels to me.

Like if I’m near a scaffold, a roof edge, or one of those upper platforms, even when I can see the rails are there and I know, logically, the setup is supposed to be safe, my head still goes straight to, “I’m going to go over.” It’s not so much about people seeing me or judging me, I don’t think. It’s more that feeling like I’m right on the edge of something bad happening, even when I know I shouldn’t be.

Even when I can see the rails are there and I know the setup is supposed to be safe.  \n\nI know that probably sounds irrational, but it’s gotten hard enough that I figured I should come talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Is it only heights that trigger this feeling, or are there other situations that make you feel like you're in danger?\n\nPatient: It’s really just the heights, as far as I can tell. Like scaffolds, roof edges, upper platforms — that kind of thing. I can look at the railings and know, logically, that they’re there and it’s supposed to be safe, but my head still jumps to, “I’m going to go over.” I don’t really get that same danger feeling in other situations, not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Has this fear of heights started to affect your work or daily activities in any way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’ve tried to keep it from being a big deal, but it is starting to show at work. I’ve missed or put off some of the upper-level walk-throughs, and that’s caused some issues with the crews and subcontractors. They’ve started questioning some of my calls, which is… not great.\n\nAnd, um, my employer’s insurer actually pushed for an occupational health review because I wasn’t completing some key safety inspections. I don’t want it to sound like I’m not doing my job, but that’s kind of where it’s gotten.\n\nDoctor: When you're in a situation that triggers this fear of heights, do you ever have episodes where you feel an intense fear or discomfort that reaches a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t want to overstate it, but it can come on pretty fast. Like the second I step onto temporary stairs, or even just start walking toward an open upper floor, my chest tightens up and my hands get sweaty.\n\nSometimes it’s like my legs are going to lock before I can make myself take another step. It usually feels like it spikes right away, within a few minutes at most.\n\nDoctor: When you're in a situation that triggers this fear, are you worried about falling or is it more about losing control of yourself?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly about falling, I think. Or… not exactly falling because something actually fails, but my mind goes straight to, “I’m going to go over,” even if I can see the rails are there and I know it’s supposed to be safe.\n\nI don’t really want to sound dramatic, but near a scaffold or roof edge or an upper platform, that’s the thought that hits me. Like I’m about to go over.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you've ever had what you would describe as a panic attack in these situations near heights?\n\nPatient: I guess… yeah, maybe. I don’t think it’s really about anything else.\n\nLike, if I’m near a scaffold, a roof edge, or an upper platform, my head just tells me, “You’re about to go over,” even when I can see the rails are there and I know it’s supposed to be safe. I know that probably doesn’t sound totally rational, but that’s where my mind goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself able to be near heights without this fear occurring?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, I try to tell myself I’m fine, especially because I should be used to job sites by now. But the second I step onto temporary stairs or even start walking toward an open upper floor, it kind of kicks in.\n\nMy chest tightens, my hands get sweaty, and I get this feeling like my legs might lock up before I can take another step. I know that probably sounds like a lot, but that’s usually what happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you actively avoid situations that involve heights because of this fear?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, I guess. I try not to make a big thing of it, but I’ve been… sort of working around it.\n\nLike, I’ll hand off height inspections to one of my foremen if I can, or move a meeting down to ground level instead of having it up on an open floor. Even when I can see the rails are there and I know the setup is supposed to be safe.  \n\nI know that probably sounds irrational, but it’s gotten hard enough that I figured I should come talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Is it only heights that trigger this feeling, or are there other situations that make you feel like you're in danger?\n\nPatient: It’s really just the heights, as far as I can tell. Like scaffolds, roof edges, upper platforms — that kind of thing. I can look at the railings and know, logically, that they’re there and it’s supposed to be safe, but my head still jumps to, “I’m going to go over.” I don’t really get that same danger feeling in other situations, not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Has this fear of heights started to affect your work or daily activities in any way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’ve tried to keep it from being a big deal, but it is starting to show at work. I’ve missed or put off some of the upper-level walk-throughs, and that’s caused some issues with the crews and subcontractors. They’ve started questioning some of my calls, which is… not great.\n\nAnd, um, my employer’s insurer actually pushed for an occupational health review because I wasn’t completing some key safety inspections. I don’t want it to sound like I’m not doing my job, but that’s kind of where it’s gotten.\n\nDoctor: When you're in a situation that triggers this fear of heights, do you ever have episodes where you feel an intense fear or discomfort that reaches a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t want to overstate it, but it can come on pretty fast. Like the second I step onto temporary stairs, or even just start walking toward an open upper floor, my chest tightens up and my hands get sweaty.\n\nSometimes it’s like my legs are going to lock before I can make myself take another step. It usually feels like it spikes right away, within a few minutes at most.\n\nDoctor: When you're in a situation that triggers this fear, are you worried about falling or is it more about losing control of yourself?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly about falling, I think. Or… not exactly falling because something actually fails, but my mind goes straight to, “I’m going to go over,” even if I can see the rails are there and I know it’s supposed to be safe.\n\nI don’t really want to sound dramatic, but near a scaffold or roof edge or an upper platform, that’s the thought that hits me. Like I’m about to go over.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you've ever had what you would describe as a panic attack in these situations near heights?\n\nPatient: I guess… yeah, maybe. I don’t know if I’d call it that exactly, but it feels pretty close.\n\nThe second I step onto temporary stairs, or even just start walking toward an open upper floor, my chest tightens up, my hands get wet, and I get this feeling like my legs might lock up before I can make myself take another step. I’m not trying to be dramatic, but it can hit really fast.\n\nDoctor: Is the fear you experience near heights ever about something other than falling?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s falling… or that feeling like I’m going to go over. I don’t think it’s really about anything else.\n\nLike, if I’m near a scaffold, a roof edge, or an upper platform, my head just tells me, “You’re about to go over,” even when I can see the rails are there and I know it’s supposed to be safe. I know that probably doesn’t sound totally rational, but that’s where my mind goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself able to be near heights without this fear occurring?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, I try to tell myself I’m fine, especially because I should be used to job sites by now. But the second I step onto temporary stairs or even start walking toward an open upper floor, it kind of kicks in.\n\nMy chest tightens, my hands get sweaty, and I get this feeling like my legs might lock up before I can take another step. I know that probably sounds like a lot, but that’s usually what happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you actively avoid situations that involve heights because of this fear?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, I guess. I try not to make a big thing of it, but I’ve been… sort of working around it.\n\nLike, I’ll hand off height inspections to one of my foremen if I can, or move a meeting down to ground level instead of having it up on an open floor. And if I really have to go up, I usually keep someone right beside me and just get through it as fast as I can.\n\nI know that probably isn’t ideal for my job, but I’m still getting the work done… or trying to, anyway.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this fear of heights?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over a year, I guess. Since that stair tower accident — about thirteen months ago now.\n\nI kept thinking it would settle down on its own, so I probably didn’t treat it like a big thing at first, but yeah… that’s when it started.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned a stair tower accident about thirteen months ago, did that incident trigger your fear of heights?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to overstate it, but that’s really when it started.\n\nBefore that accident, heights were just part of the job. Since then — for about thirteen months now — it’s been different. I kept hoping it was just a phase or something I’d get over, but it’s been going on since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time, lasting at least a few months, where you didn't experience this fear of heights?\n\nPatient: No… not really. I mean, I wish I could say there was a stretch where it went away, but since that stair tower accident, it’s kind of just been there.\n\nMaybe some days are easier than others, but I don’t think I’ve had a few months where I wasn’t dealing with it. It’s been about thirteen months now.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of heights been present for most days since it started about thirteen months ago?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s fair to say. Most days, yes.\n\nI don’t like making it sound bigger than it is, but since that stair tower accident about thirteen months ago, it’s been pretty consistently there. Some days I manage around it better, but I don’t think it’s really gone away.\n\nDoctor: Is there ever a part of your fear of heights that isn't about falling, but maybe about something else like being in a situation you can't control or being seen by others?\n\nPatient: I think… mostly it’s about falling, honestly. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not totally sure how to separate it, to be honest.

It’s definitely more noticeable at work, because that’s where I’m around ladders, platforms, unfinished edges, that kind of thing. Outside of work… I try not to put myself in those situations much, so I don’t have as many examples. I wouldn’t want to overstate it.

But I guess if I’m near a height somewhere else, I can still feel that same sort of reaction. Maybe not as often, just because it doesn’t come up as much. Does that answer what you mean?

I work construction, and lately when I have to get near a scaffold, or a roof edge, or one of the upper platforms, my head just kind of tells me I’m about to go over. Even when I can see the rails are there and I know the setup is supposed to be safe.  \n\nI know that probably sounds irrational, but it’s gotten hard enough that I figured I should come talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Is it only heights that trigger this feeling, or are there other situations that make you feel like you're in danger?\n\nPatient: It’s really just the heights, as far as I can tell. Like scaffolds, roof edges, upper platforms — that kind of thing. I can look at the railings and know, logically, that they’re there and it’s supposed to be safe, but my head still jumps to, “I’m going to go over.” I don’t really get that same danger feeling in other situations, not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Has this fear of heights started to affect your work or daily activities in any way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’ve tried to keep it from being a big deal, but it is starting to show at work. I’ve missed or put off some of the upper-level walk-throughs, and that’s caused some issues with the crews and subcontractors. They’ve started questioning some of my calls, which is… not great.\n\nAnd, um, my employer’s insurer actually pushed for an occupational health review because I wasn’t completing some key safety inspections. I don’t want it to sound like I’m not doing my job, but that’s kind of where it’s gotten.\n\nDoctor: When you're in a situation that triggers this fear of heights, do you ever have episodes where you feel an intense fear or discomfort that reaches a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t want to overstate it, but it can come on pretty fast. Like the second I step onto temporary stairs, or even just start walking toward an open upper floor, my chest tightens up and my hands get sweaty.\n\nSometimes it’s like my legs are going to lock before I can make myself take another step. It usually feels like it spikes right away, within a few minutes at most.\n\nDoctor: When you're in a situation that triggers this fear, are you worried about falling or is it more about losing control of yourself?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly about falling, I think. Or… not exactly falling because something actually fails, but my mind goes straight to, “I’m going to go over,” even if I can see the rails are there and I know it’s supposed to be safe.\n\nI don’t really want to sound dramatic, but near a scaffold or roof edge or an upper platform, that’s the thought that hits me. Like I’m about to go over.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you've ever had what you would describe as a panic attack in these situations near heights?\n\nPatient: I guess… yeah, maybe. I don’t know if I’d call it that exactly, but it feels pretty close.\n\nThe second I step onto temporary stairs, or even just start walking toward an open upper floor, my chest tightens up, my hands get wet, and I get this feeling like my legs might lock up before I can make myself take another step. I’m not trying to be dramatic, but it can hit really fast.\n\nDoctor: Is the fear you experience near heights ever about something other than falling?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s falling… or that feeling like I’m going to go over. I don’t think it’s really about anything else.\n\nLike, if I’m near a scaffold, a roof edge, or an upper platform, my head just tells me, “You’re about to go over,” even when I can see the rails are there and I know it’s supposed to be safe. I know that probably doesn’t sound totally rational, but that’s where my mind goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself able to be near heights without this fear occurring?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, I try to tell myself I’m fine, especially because I should be used to job sites by now. But the second I step onto temporary stairs or even start walking toward an open upper floor, it kind of kicks in.\n\nMy chest tightens, my hands get sweaty, and I get this feeling like my legs might lock up before I can take another step. I know that probably sounds like a lot, but that’s usually what happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you actively avoid situations that involve heights because of this fear?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, I guess. I try not to make a big thing of it, but I’ve been… sort of working around it.\n\nLike, I’ll hand off height inspections to one of my foremen if I can, or move a meeting down to ground level instead of having it up on an open floor. And if I really have to go up, I usually keep someone right beside me and just get through it as fast as I can.\n\nI know that probably isn’t ideal for my job, but I’m still getting the work done… or trying to, anyway.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this fear of heights?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over a year, I guess. Since that stair tower accident — about thirteen months ago now.\n\nI kept thinking it would settle down on its own, so I probably didn’t treat it like a big thing at first, but yeah… that’s when it started.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned a stair tower accident about thirteen months ago, did that incident trigger your fear of heights?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to overstate it, but that’s really when it started.\n\nBefore that accident, heights were just part of the job. Since then — for about thirteen months now — it’s been different. I kept hoping it was just a phase or something I’d get over, but it’s been going on since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time, lasting at least a few months, where you didn't experience this fear of heights?\n\nPatient: No… not really. I mean, I wish I could say there was a stretch where it went away, but since that stair tower accident, it’s kind of just been there.\n\nMaybe some days are easier than others, but I don’t think I’ve had a few months where I wasn’t dealing with it. It’s been about thirteen months now.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of heights been present for most days since it started about thirteen months ago?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s fair to say. Most days, yes.\n\nI don’t like making it sound bigger than it is, but since that stair tower accident about thirteen months ago, it’s been pretty consistently there. Some days I manage around it better, but I don’t think it’s really gone away.\n\nDoctor: Is there ever a part of your fear of heights that isn't about falling, but maybe about something else like being in a situation you can't control or being seen by others?\n\nPatient: I think… mostly it’s about falling, honestly. Or at least that’s how it feels to me.\n\nLike if I’m near a scaffold, a roof edge, or one of those upper platforms, even when I can see the rails are there and I know, logically, the setup is supposed to be safe, my head still goes straight to, “I’m going to go over.” It’s not so much about people seeing me or judging me, I don’t think. If there’s no height or edge or platform kind of situation, I haven’t really noticed the same reaction. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of heights something that you experience only at work, or does it also happen in other, non-work situations where you're near heights?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate it, to be honest.\n\nIt’s definitely more noticeable at work, because that’s where I’m around ladders, platforms, unfinished edges, that kind of thing. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think it can, outside of work too.

I guess I’ve been focusing on work because that’s where it comes up the most, but it’s really the height itself. Like if I have to get near a scaffold, or a roof edge, or some upper platform, my mind just kind of tells me I’m about to go over—even when I can see the rails are there and I know it’s supposed to be safe.

So… yes, I’d say it’s not only work. Work just makes it harder to avoid, if that makes sense.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.228, F41.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, it’s a little embarrassing, honestly. I work construction, and lately when I have to get near a scaffold, or a roof edge, or one of the upper platforms, my head just kind of tells me I’m about to go over. Even when I can see the rails are there and I know the setup is supposed to be safe.  \n\nI know that probably sounds irrational, but it’s gotten hard enough that I figured I should come talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Is it only heights that trigger this feeling, or are there other situations that make you feel like you're in danger?\n\nPatient: It’s really just the heights, as far as I can tell. Like scaffolds, roof edges, upper platforms — that kind of thing. I can look at the railings and know, logically, that they’re there and it’s supposed to be safe, but my head still jumps to, “I’m going to go over.” I don’t really get that same danger feeling in other situations, not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Has this fear of heights started to affect your work or daily activities in any way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’ve tried to keep it from being a big deal, but it is starting to show at work. I’ve missed or put off some of the upper-level walk-throughs, and that’s caused some issues with the crews and subcontractors. They’ve started questioning some of my calls, which is… not great.\n\nAnd, um, my employer’s insurer actually pushed for an occupational health review because I wasn’t completing some key safety inspections. I don’t want it to sound like I’m not doing my job, but that’s kind of where it’s gotten.\n\nDoctor: When you're in a situation that triggers this fear of heights, do you ever have episodes where you feel an intense fear or discomfort that reaches a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t want to overstate it, but it can come on pretty fast. Like the second I step onto temporary stairs, or even just start walking toward an open upper floor, my chest tightens up and my hands get sweaty.\n\nSometimes it’s like my legs are going to lock before I can make myself take another step. It usually feels like it spikes right away, within a few minutes at most.\n\nDoctor: When you're in a situation that triggers this fear, are you worried about falling or is it more about losing control of yourself?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly about falling, I think. Or… not exactly falling because something actually fails, but my mind goes straight to, “I’m going to go over,” even if I can see the rails are there and I know it’s supposed to be safe.\n\nI don’t really want to sound dramatic, but near a scaffold or roof edge or an upper platform, that’s the thought that hits me. Like I’m about to go over.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you've ever had what you would describe as a panic attack in these situations near heights?\n\nPatient: I guess… yeah, maybe. I don’t know if I’d call it that exactly, but it feels pretty close.\n\nThe second I step onto temporary stairs, or even just start walking toward an open upper floor, my chest tightens up, my hands get wet, and I get this feeling like my legs might lock up before I can make myself take another step. I’m not trying to be dramatic, but it can hit really fast.\n\nDoctor: Is the fear you experience near heights ever about something other than falling?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s falling… or that feeling like I’m going to go over. I don’t think it’s really about anything else.\n\nLike, if I’m near a scaffold, a roof edge, or an upper platform, my head just tells me, “You’re about to go over,” even when I can see the rails are there and I know it’s supposed to be safe. I know that probably doesn’t sound totally rational, but that’s where my mind goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself able to be near heights without this fear occurring?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, I try to tell myself I’m fine, especially because I should be used to job sites by now. But the second I step onto temporary stairs or even start walking toward an open upper floor, it kind of kicks in.\n\nMy chest tightens, my hands get sweaty, and I get this feeling like my legs might lock up before I can take another step. I know that probably sounds like a lot, but that’s usually what happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you actively avoid situations that involve heights because of this fear?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, I guess. I try not to make a big thing of it, but I’ve been… sort of working around it.\n\nLike, I’ll hand off height inspections to one of my foremen if I can, or move a meeting down to ground level instead of having it up on an open floor. And if I really have to go up, I usually keep someone right beside me and just get through it as fast as I can.\n\nI know that probably isn’t ideal for my job, but I’m still getting the work done… or trying to, anyway.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this fear of heights?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over a year, I guess. Since that stair tower accident — about thirteen months ago now.\n\nI kept thinking it would settle down on its own, so I probably didn’t treat it like a big thing at first, but yeah… that’s when it started.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned a stair tower accident about thirteen months ago, did that incident trigger your fear of heights?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to overstate it, but that’s really when it started.\n\nBefore that accident, heights were just part of the job. Since then — for about thirteen months now — it’s been different. I kept hoping it was just a phase or something I’d get over, but it’s been going on since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time, lasting at least a few months, where you didn't experience this fear of heights?\n\nPatient: No… not really. I mean, I wish I could say there was a stretch where it went away, but since that stair tower accident, it’s kind of just been there.\n\nMaybe some days are easier than others, but I don’t think I’ve had a few months where I wasn’t dealing with it. It’s been about thirteen months now.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of heights been present for most days since it started about thirteen months ago?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s fair to say. Most days, yes.\n\nI don’t like making it sound bigger than it is, but since that stair tower accident about thirteen months ago, it’s been pretty consistently there. Some days I manage around it better, but I don’t think it’s really gone away.\n\nDoctor: Is there ever a part of your fear of heights that isn't about falling, but maybe about something else like being in a situation you can't control or being seen by others?\n\nPatient: I think… mostly it’s about falling, honestly. Or at least that’s how it feels to me.\n\nLike if I’m near a scaffold, a roof edge, or one of those upper platforms, even when I can see the rails are there and I know, logically, the setup is supposed to be safe, my head still goes straight to, “I’m going to go over.” It’s not so much about people seeing me or judging me, I don’t think. It’s more that feeling like I’m right on the edge of something bad happening, even when I know I shouldn’t be.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing anxiety or fear in situations that don't involve heights?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in any big way, anyway.\n\nI mean, I get stressed like anybody, work stuff and normal life stuff, but the fear that really grabs me is when heights are involved. If there’s no height or edge or platform kind of situation, I haven’t really noticed the same reaction. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of heights something that you experience only at work, or does it also happen in other, non-work situations where you're near heights?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate it, to be honest.\n\nIt’s definitely more noticeable at work, because that’s where I’m around ladders, platforms, unfinished edges, that kind of thing. Outside of work… I try not to put myself in those situations much, so I don’t have as many examples. I wouldn’t want to overstate it.\n\nBut I guess if I’m near a height somewhere else, I can still feel that same sort of reaction. Maybe not as often, just because it doesn’t come up as much. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of heights occur in your personal life as well, outside of work situations?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it can, outside of work too.\n\nI guess I’ve been focusing on work because that’s where it comes up the most, but it’s really the height itself. Like if I have to get near a scaffold, or a roof edge, or some upper platform, my mind just kind of tells me I’m about to go over—even when I can see the rails are there and I know it’s supposed to be safe.\n\nSo… yes, I’d say it’s not only work. Work just makes it harder to avoid, if that makes sense.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Outside of work… I try not to put myself in those situations much, so I don’t have as many examples. I wouldn’t want to overstate it.\n\nBut I guess if I’m near a height somewhere else, I can still feel that same sort of reaction. Maybe not as often, just because it doesn’t come up as much. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of heights occur in your personal life as well, outside of work situations?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it can, outside of work too.\n\nI guess I’ve been focusing on work because that’s where it comes up the most, but it’s really the height itself. Like if I have to get near a scaffold, or a roof edge, or some upper platform, my mind just kind of tells me I’m about to go over—even when I can see the rails are there and I know it’s supposed to be safe.\n\nSo… yes, I’d say it’s not only work. Work just makes it harder to avoid, if that makes sense.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you experienced significant anxiety or fear in situations unrelated to heights over the past year?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 44 / construction_manager / associates\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really, no… at least not anything I’d call significant.

I mean, I’ve had normal stress here and there, but when I think about real fear or anxiety that feels out of proportion, it’s been tied to heights. I don’t want to give the wrong impression, but I haven’t noticed that same kind of reaction in unrelated situations.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, it’s a little embarrassing, honestly. I work construction, and lately when I have to get near a scaffold, or a roof edge, or one of the upper platforms, my head just kind of tells me I’m about to go over. Even when I can see the rails are there and I know the setup is supposed to be safe.  \n\nI know that probably sounds irrational, but it’s gotten hard enough that I figured I should come talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Is it only heights that trigger this feeling, or are there other situations that make you feel like you're in danger?\n\nPatient: It’s really just the heights, as far as I can tell. Like scaffolds, roof edges, upper platforms — that kind of thing. I can look at the railings and know, logically, that they’re there and it’s supposed to be safe, but my head still jumps to, “I’m going to go over.” I don’t really get that same danger feeling in other situations, not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Has this fear of heights started to affect your work or daily activities in any way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’ve tried to keep it from being a big deal, but it is starting to show at work. I’ve missed or put off some of the upper-level walk-throughs, and that’s caused some issues with the crews and subcontractors. They’ve started questioning some of my calls, which is… not great.\n\nAnd, um, my employer’s insurer actually pushed for an occupational health review because I wasn’t completing some key safety inspections. I don’t want it to sound like I’m not doing my job, but that’s kind of where it’s gotten.\n\nDoctor: When you're in a situation that triggers this fear of heights, do you ever have episodes where you feel an intense fear or discomfort that reaches a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t want to overstate it, but it can come on pretty fast. Like the second I step onto temporary stairs, or even just start walking toward an open upper floor, my chest tightens up and my hands get sweaty.\n\nSometimes it’s like my legs are going to lock before I can make myself take another step. It usually feels like it spikes right away, within a few minutes at most.\n\nDoctor: When you're in a situation that triggers this fear, are you worried about falling or is it more about losing control of yourself?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly about falling, I think. Or… not exactly falling because something actually fails, but my mind goes straight to, “I’m going to go over,” even if I can see the rails are there and I know it’s supposed to be safe.\n\nI don’t really want to sound dramatic, but near a scaffold or roof edge or an upper platform, that’s the thought that hits me. Like I’m about to go over.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you've ever had what you would describe as a panic attack in these situations near heights?\n\nPatient: I guess… yeah, maybe. I don’t know if I’d call it that exactly, but it feels pretty close.\n\nThe second I step onto temporary stairs, or even just start walking toward an open upper floor, my chest tightens up, my hands get wet, and I get this feeling like my legs might lock up before I can make myself take another step. I’m not trying to be dramatic, but it can hit really fast.\n\nDoctor: Is the fear you experience near heights ever about something other than falling?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s falling… or that feeling like I’m going to go over. I don’t think it’s really about anything else.\n\nLike, if I’m near a scaffold, a roof edge, or an upper platform, my head just tells me, “You’re about to go over,” even when I can see the rails are there and I know it’s supposed to be safe. I know that probably doesn’t sound totally rational, but that’s where my mind goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself able to be near heights without this fear occurring?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, I try to tell myself I’m fine, especially because I should be used to job sites by now. But the second I step onto temporary stairs or even start walking toward an open upper floor, it kind of kicks in.\n\nMy chest tightens, my hands get sweaty, and I get this feeling like my legs might lock up before I can take another step. I know that probably sounds like a lot, but that’s usually what happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you actively avoid situations that involve heights because of this fear?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, I guess. I try not to make a big thing of it, but I’ve been… sort of working around it.\n\nLike, I’ll hand off height inspections to one of my foremen if I can, or move a meeting down to ground level instead of having it up on an open floor. And if I really have to go up, I usually keep someone right beside me and just get through it as fast as I can.\n\nI know that probably isn’t ideal for my job, but I’m still getting the work done… or trying to, anyway.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this fear of heights?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over a year, I guess. Since that stair tower accident — about thirteen months ago now.\n\nI kept thinking it would settle down on its own, so I probably didn’t treat it like a big thing at first, but yeah… that’s when it started.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned a stair tower accident about thirteen months ago, did that incident trigger your fear of heights?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to overstate it, but that’s really when it started.\n\nBefore that accident, heights were just part of the job. Since then — for about thirteen months now — it’s been different. I kept hoping it was just a phase or something I’d get over, but it’s been going on since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time, lasting at least a few months, where you didn't experience this fear of heights?\n\nPatient: No… not really. I mean, I wish I could say there was a stretch where it went away, but since that stair tower accident, it’s kind of just been there.\n\nMaybe some days are easier than others, but I don’t think I’ve had a few months where I wasn’t dealing with it. It’s been about thirteen months now.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of heights been present for most days since it started about thirteen months ago?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s fair to say. Most days, yes.\n\nI don’t like making it sound bigger than it is, but since that stair tower accident about thirteen months ago, it’s been pretty consistently there. Some days I manage around it better, but I don’t think it’s really gone away.\n\nDoctor: Is there ever a part of your fear of heights that isn't about falling, but maybe about something else like being in a situation you can't control or being seen by others?\n\nPatient: I think… mostly it’s about falling, honestly. Or at least that’s how it feels to me.\n\nLike if I’m near a scaffold, a roof edge, or one of those upper platforms, even when I can see the rails are there and I know, logically, the setup is supposed to be safe, my head still goes straight to, “I’m going to go over.” It’s not so much about people seeing me or judging me, I don’t think. It’s more that feeling like I’m right on the edge of something bad happening, even when I know I shouldn’t be.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing anxiety or fear in situations that don't involve heights?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in any big way, anyway.\n\nI mean, I get stressed like anybody, work stuff and normal life stuff, but the fear that really grabs me is when heights are involved. If there’s no height or edge or platform kind of situation, I haven’t really noticed the same reaction. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of heights something that you experience only at work, or does it also happen in other, non-work situations where you're near heights?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate it, to be honest.\n\nIt’s definitely more noticeable at work, because that’s where I’m around ladders, platforms, unfinished edges, that kind of thing. Outside of work… I try not to put myself in those situations much, so I don’t have as many examples. I wouldn’t want to overstate it.\n\nBut I guess if I’m near a height somewhere else, I can still feel that same sort of reaction. Maybe not as often, just because it doesn’t come up as much. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of heights occur in your personal life as well, outside of work situations?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it can, outside of work too.\n\nI guess I’ve been focusing on work because that’s where it comes up the most, but it’s really the height itself. Like if I have to get near a scaffold, or a roof edge, or some upper platform, my mind just kind of tells me I’m about to go over—even when I can see the rails are there and I know it’s supposed to be safe.\n\nSo… yes, I’d say it’s not only work. Work just makes it harder to avoid, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant anxiety or fear in situations unrelated to heights over the past year?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… at least not anything I’d call significant.\n\nI mean, I’ve had normal stress here and there, but when I think about real fear or anxiety that feels out of proportion, it’s been tied to heights. I work construction, and lately when I have to get near a scaffold, or a roof edge, or one of the upper platforms, my head just kind of tells me I’m about to go over. Even when I can see the rails are there and I know the setup is supposed to be safe.  \n\nI know that probably sounds irrational, but it’s gotten hard enough that I figured I should come talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Is it only heights that trigger this feeling, or are there other situations that make you feel like you're in danger?\n\nPatient: It’s really just the heights, as far as I can tell. Like scaffolds, roof edges, upper platforms — that kind of thing. I can look at the railings and know, logically, that they’re there and it’s supposed to be safe, but my head still jumps to, “I’m going to go over.” I don’t really get that same danger feeling in other situations, not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Has this fear of heights started to affect your work or daily activities in any way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’ve tried to keep it from being a big deal, but it is starting to show at work. I’ve missed or put off some of the upper-level walk-throughs, and that’s caused some issues with the crews and subcontractors. They’ve started questioning some of my calls, which is… not great.\n\nAnd, um, my employer’s insurer actually pushed for an occupational health review because I wasn’t completing some key safety inspections. I don’t want it to sound like I’m not doing my job, but that’s kind of where it’s gotten.\n\nDoctor: When you're in a situation that triggers this fear of heights, do you ever have episodes where you feel an intense fear or discomfort that reaches a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t want to overstate it, but it can come on pretty fast. Like the second I step onto temporary stairs, or even just start walking toward an open upper floor, my chest tightens up and my hands get sweaty.\n\nSometimes it’s like my legs are going to lock before I can make myself take another step. It usually feels like it spikes right away, within a few minutes at most.\n\nDoctor: When you're in a situation that triggers this fear, are you worried about falling or is it more about losing control of yourself?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly about falling, I think. Or… not exactly falling because something actually fails, but my mind goes straight to, “I’m going to go over,” even if I can see the rails are there and I know it’s supposed to be safe.\n\nI don’t really want to sound dramatic, but near a scaffold or roof edge or an upper platform, that’s the thought that hits me. Like I’m about to go over.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you've ever had what you would describe as a panic attack in these situations near heights?\n\nPatient: I guess… yeah, maybe. I don’t know if I’d call it that exactly, but it feels pretty close.\n\nThe second I step onto temporary stairs, or even just start walking toward an open upper floor, my chest tightens up, my hands get wet, and I get this feeling like my legs might lock up before I can make myself take another step. I’m not trying to be dramatic, but it can hit really fast.\n\nDoctor: Is the fear you experience near heights ever about something other than falling?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s falling… or that feeling like I’m going to go over. I don’t think it’s really about anything else.\n\nLike, if I’m near a scaffold, a roof edge, or an upper platform, my head just tells me, “You’re about to go over,” even when I can see the rails are there and I know it’s supposed to be safe. I know that probably doesn’t sound totally rational, but that’s where my mind goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself able to be near heights without this fear occurring?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, I try to tell myself I’m fine, especially because I should be used to job sites by now. But the second I step onto temporary stairs or even start walking toward an open upper floor, it kind of kicks in.\n\nMy chest tightens, my hands get sweaty, and I get this feeling like my legs might lock up before I can take another step. I know that probably sounds like a lot, but that’s usually what happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you actively avoid situations that involve heights because of this fear?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, I guess. I try not to make a big thing of it, but I’ve been… sort of working around it.\n\nLike, I’ll hand off height inspections to one of my foremen if I can, or move a meeting down to ground level instead of having it up on an open floor. And if I really have to go up, I usually keep someone right beside me and just get through it as fast as I can.\n\nI know that probably isn’t ideal for my job, but I’m still getting the work done… or trying to, anyway.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this fear of heights?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over a year, I guess. Since that stair tower accident — about thirteen months ago now.\n\nI kept thinking it would settle down on its own, so I probably didn’t treat it like a big thing at first, but yeah… that’s when it started.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned a stair tower accident about thirteen months ago, did that incident trigger your fear of heights?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to overstate it, but that’s really when it started.\n\nBefore that accident, heights were just part of the job. Since then — for about thirteen months now — it’s been different. I kept hoping it was just a phase or something I’d get over, but it’s been going on since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time, lasting at least a few months, where you didn't experience this fear of heights?\n\nPatient: No… not really. I mean, I wish I could say there was a stretch where it went away, but since that stair tower accident, it’s kind of just been there.\n\nMaybe some days are easier than others, but I don’t think I’ve had a few months where I wasn’t dealing with it. It’s been about thirteen months now.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of heights been present for most days since it started about thirteen months ago?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s fair to say. I think it’s really just the heights, yeah… at least that’s the only thing that feels significant to me.

I’ve missed or put off some of the upper-level walk-throughs, and that’s caused some issues with the crews and subcontractors. They’ve started questioning some of my calls, which is… not great.\n\nAnd, um, my employer’s insurer actually pushed for an occupational health review because I wasn’t completing some key safety inspections. I don’t want it to sound like I’m not doing my job, but that’s kind of where it’s gotten.\n\nDoctor: When you're in a situation that triggers this fear of heights, do you ever have episodes where you feel an intense fear or discomfort that reaches a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It usually feels like it spikes right away, within a few minutes at most.\n\nDoctor: When you're in a situation that triggers this fear, are you worried about falling or is it more about losing control of yourself?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly about falling, I think. Or… not exactly falling because something actually fails, but my mind goes straight to, “I’m going to go over,” even if I can see the rails are there and I know it’s supposed to be safe.\n\nI don’t really want to sound dramatic, but near a scaffold or roof edge or an upper platform, that’s the thought that hits me. Like I’m about to go over.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you've ever had what you would describe as a panic attack in these situations near heights?\n\nPatient: I guess… yeah, maybe. I don’t know if I’d call it that exactly, but it feels pretty close.\n\nThe second I step onto temporary stairs, or even just start walking toward an open upper floor, my chest tightens up, my hands get wet, and I get this feeling like my legs might lock up before I can make myself take another step. I’m not trying to be dramatic, but it can hit really fast.\n\nDoctor: Is the fear you experience near heights ever about something other than falling?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s falling… or that feeling like I’m going to go over. I don’t think it’s really about anything else.\n\nLike, if I’m near a scaffold, a roof edge, or an upper platform, my head just tells me, “You’re about to go over,” even when I can see the rails are there and I know it’s supposed to be safe. I know that probably doesn’t sound totally rational, but that’s where my mind goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself able to be near heights without this fear occurring?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, I try to tell myself I’m fine, especially because I should be used to job sites by now. But the second I step onto temporary stairs or even start walking toward an open upper floor, it kind of kicks in.\n\nMy chest tightens, my hands get sweaty, and I get this feeling like my legs might lock up before I can take another step. I know that probably sounds like a lot, but that’s usually what happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you actively avoid situations that involve heights because of this fear?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, I guess. I try not to make a big thing of it, but I’ve been… sort of working around it.\n\nLike, I’ll hand off height inspections to one of my foremen if I can, or move a meeting down to ground level instead of having it up on an open floor. And if I really have to go up, I usually keep someone right beside me and just get through it as fast as I can.\n\nI know that probably isn’t ideal for my job, but I’m still getting the work done… or trying to, anyway.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this fear of heights?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over a year, I guess. Since that stair tower accident — about thirteen months ago now.\n\nI kept thinking it would settle down on its own, so I probably didn’t treat it like a big thing at first, but yeah… that’s when it started.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned a stair tower accident about thirteen months ago, did that incident trigger your fear of heights?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to overstate it, but that’s really when it started.\n\nBefore that accident, heights were just part of the job. Since then — for about thirteen months now — it’s been different. I kept hoping it was just a phase or something I’d get over, but it’s been going on since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time, lasting at least a few months, where you didn't experience this fear of heights?\n\nPatient: No… not really. I mean, I wish I could say there was a stretch where it went away, but since that stair tower accident, it’s kind of just been there.\n\nMaybe some days are easier than others, but I don’t think I’ve had a few months where I wasn’t dealing with it. It’s been about thirteen months now.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of heights been present for most days since it started about thirteen months ago?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s fair to say. Most days, yes.\n\nI don’t like making it sound bigger than it is, but since that stair tower accident about thirteen months ago, it’s been pretty consistently there. Some days I manage around it better, but I don’t think it’s really gone away.\n\nDoctor: Is there ever a part of your fear of heights that isn't about falling, but maybe about something else like being in a situation you can't control or being seen by others?\n\nPatient: I think… mostly it’s about falling, honestly. Or at least that’s how it feels to me.\n\nLike if I’m near a scaffold, a roof edge, or one of those upper platforms, even when I can see the rails are there and I know, logically, the setup is supposed to be safe, my head still goes straight to, “I’m going to go over.” It’s not so much about people seeing me or judging me, I don’t think. It’s more that feeling like I’m right on the edge of something bad happening, even when I know I shouldn’t be.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing anxiety or fear in situations that don't involve heights?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in any big way, anyway.\n\nI mean, I get stressed like anybody, work stuff and normal life stuff, but the fear that really grabs me is when heights are involved. If there’s no height or edge or platform kind of situation, I haven’t really noticed the same reaction. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of heights something that you experience only at work, or does it also happen in other, non-work situations where you're near heights?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate it, to be honest.\n\nIt’s definitely more noticeable at work, because that’s where I’m around ladders, platforms, unfinished edges, that kind of thing. Outside of work… I try not to put myself in those situations much, so I don’t have as many examples. I wouldn’t want to overstate it.\n\nBut I guess if I’m near a height somewhere else, I can still feel that same sort of reaction. Maybe not as often, just because it doesn’t come up as much. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of heights occur in your personal life as well, outside of work situations?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it can, outside of work too.\n\nI guess I’ve been focusing on work because that’s where it comes up the most, but it’s really the height itself. Like if I have to get near a scaffold, or a roof edge, or some upper platform, my mind just kind of tells me I’m about to go over—even when I can see the rails are there and I know it’s supposed to be safe.\n\nSo… yes, I’d say it’s not only work. Work just makes it harder to avoid, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant anxiety or fear in situations unrelated to heights over the past year?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… at least not anything I’d call significant.\n\nI mean, I’ve had normal stress here and there, but when I think about real fear or anxiety that feels out of proportion, it’s been tied to heights.
